with 100 groups and 900 DMs, `u0500` with 500 follows, and the 100,015
messages of the retained 0.0.25 replay database. The same fixture for every
track and for the control.

**Harness and judge.** [`benchmarks/protocol.md`](../benchmarks/protocol.md);
the same three scorers as the 2026-09-05 run (gpt-6-astra, grok-4.6,
# LLP 2000.000: Snapback 3 — keep the core, flip two substrates, start at hub scale

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Draft
**Role:** Second opinion on LLP 2000; since 2026-09-12 also the design brief Snapback 4 builds (LLP 3000)
**Systems:** Snapback 3 product architecture, bounded computation, client data model, client API vocabulary, language and tooling, effects runtime, verification (simulation), performance budgets, process
**Author:** Claude Fable 5.1, at Charlie Cheever's request
**Date:** 2026-09-12
**Revised:** 2026-09-12 (adopted as the Snapback 4 design brief by LLP 3000 decision 3); 2026-09-12 (initial)
**Related:** [LLP 2000](./2000-snapback3.rfc.md) (parent), [LLP 3000](./3000-snapback4.explainer.md) (the Snapback 4 root that builds this brief), LLP 0000, [LLP 0329](./0329-agent-native-rebuild-diagnosis.research.md), [LLP 0329.014.004](./0329.014.004-bounded-data-programs-v1-design.rfc.md), [LLP 0329.016](./0329.016-agentic-dev-loop-from-scratch.rfc.md), [LLP 1000](./1000-snapback2.explainer.md), [LLP 1001](./1001-query-engine.spec.md), [LLP 1002](./1002-dev-loop.spec.md), [LLP 1012](./1012-local-first-v1.spec.md), [LLP 1018.003](./1018.003-truthful-and-composed-client.decision.md), [LLP 1018.008](./1018.008-elegance-after-the-corrections.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.007](./1018.008.007-affordable-offline-acquisition.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.008](./1018.008.008-locality-of-application-changes.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.009](./1018.008.009-residual-ergonomic-gaps.research.md), [LLP 1019](./1019-derived-delivery-custody.rfc.md), [LLP 1020](./1020-day-two-evolution-and-legible-ceilings.rfc.md), [LLP 1023](./1023-clique-builder-jump.rfc.md), [LLP 1024](./1024-single-lane-delivered-search.rfc.md), [LLP 1025](./1025-digest-final-emission-at-hub-scale.research.md), `rules/RULES.md`, `rules/NOT-DOING.md`, `benchmarks/runs/2026-09-05-clique-full-3x3/holistic-scores.md`

## 1. Summary

LLP 2000 is right that the whole application is the unit of design, right
that ownership comes before abstraction, and right to ask for deterministic
simulation and an early real deployment. Its weakness is that it treats the
two structural choices that produced Snapback 2's costs as open research
questions, and then proposes a first experiment at exactly the scale where
those costs are invisible. This document takes a position on both, names
what to keep from Snapback 2 verbatim, and rewrites the first slice so it
can fail for the right reasons.

The position, in one paragraph. Snapback 2's *core* is excellent and should
be copied: the rule language and its uniformity analysis, the structural-fact
family, one data language, typed states with no silence, refusals that paste
a rewrite, personas, the successor classifier, the derived per-recipient
log, the loop verbs and their sub-second budgets. Two *substrates* under
that core are where the record shows the hours went, and both should be
flipped rather than re-investigated: (1) the static certificate should keep
certifying **authorization and shape** and stop pricing **bytes and steps**,
which move to a deterministic runtime meter with honest `capped`/abort
outcomes; and (2) the client should hold **a replica of the viewer's declared
partition** — the local-first shape every shipped sync engine and Snapback
2's own `deliver` log converge on — instead of a per-query result cache
proven by receipts. Those two flips dissolve most of the machinery that
leaked into the application (receipts, coverage, halos, validators, grants,
ID slices, drains, `withHistory`, follower inboxes, per-table retention
words) and turn the 28-second acquisition into a bulk stream whose floor is
the device's write speed. The first slice should be built on the **hub
fixture** (1,000 memberships, 500 follows, 100,000 messages), with the
day-two change designed before the day-one screen, a native client in the
first slice, and a deterministic simulator as the primary test harness.

Three claims this document makes that LLP 2000 does not:

1. **The costs have two named causes, and both are decisions, not
   mysteries.** Byte-priced static certification and the receipt-proven
   result cache account for every ergonomic residual in LLP 1018.008.009
   except documentation. §2 shows the mapping.
2. **The first experiment must include the hub.** Snapback 2 was "elegant on
   day one and hostile on day two" (LLP 1020) and broke at 1,000 memberships
   (LLPs 1023, 1024, 1025). A two-principal slice will pass and teach nothing.
3. **The process is part of the product.** Seventy-one LLPs, over two
   thousand commits, and seven ceiling raises in fourteen days produced a
   sound system nobody can hold in their head. Snapback 3 needs one owner on
   the core, a simulator instead of a test matrix, and budgets anchored to
   competitors so that raising one means admitting a loss.

## 2. What the record says, read as causes

The evidence LLP 2000 §2 cites is right; its reading is too gentle. Every
row below is a Snapback 2 cost recorded in the corpus, with the substrate
that produced it. Numbers are the corpus's own and retain its scope limits.

| Recorded cost | Where | Cause |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Adding `editedAt: time ?` (44 bytes × 244 retained copies = 10,736) pushed `messages.searchAll` to 4,199,688 closure bytes against 4,194,304; the builder shrank a page from 20 to 19 | LLP 1018.008.008; 0.0.21 day-two diary | **Static byte pricing.** Closure bytes are a bound on what a receipt could ship; the product paid for the bound, not the work |
| The 1,000-membership digest fold certifies at 9,922,698 heap and 8,691,704 closure bytes and is refused; the recipient-keyed form is admitted at 2,733,253 / 1,848,418 | LLP 1025 §3 | **Static byte pricing** (heap = steps × 32 + closure scratch). A runtime meter would have run the fold and reported actual bytes |
| A message page is 50, not 200, because a row "prices at 16,016 bytes" and 200 exceed the 1 MiB output ceiling | LLP 1012 §3 | **Static byte pricing** of the worst-case row width |
| A per-recipient search projection is refused at 20,863,641 reserved steps against 1,000,000 | LLP 1024 §1 | **Static step pricing** of a derivation the engine would perform incrementally |
| Full acquisition of 10,008 messages: 67.4 s → 53.6 s → 32.6 s → 28.4 s against a 2–3 s objective; 201 requests, 200 acknowledgement mutations, 612 IndexedDB transactions; PowerSync reports 10,000 rows in 0.36 s native and 2.5 s on web | LLP 1018.008.007; LLP 1018.008.009 §4 | **Result cache, not replica.** Every 50-row page is a certified query, a receipt, a durable acknowledgement mutation, and a store swap |
| Adding a count to a thread makes the composite `partial` offline whenever count authority is missing, so the template declares an independent `history` query and `withHistory` bindings; 27 files / 1,718 lines → 28 / 1,751 after the fix | LLP 1018.008 F2; LLP 1018.008.009 §2 | **Result cache.** The count is not a fact the receipt covers, so the client cannot compose locally |
| `shared/thread.ts` describes table identity, row selection, order, create membership, draft fields and mutation targets a second time | LLP 1018.008 F2 | **Result cache.** Items must be reconstructed from answers because there is no local table to read |
| Sending stalled with 85 IDs remaining against a certified 352-ID slice on an 8,192-ID grant; recovery needed a "Sync now" action | LLP 1018.008 F1 | **Result cache + engine-minted IDs.** Prediction needs server ids before commit, so ids are granted in slices |
| A follower browser tab writes through a durable inbox row; the SDK carries `forwarding`, `writer-unresponsive`, `inbox-full`, `follower` states | LLP 1018.004 | **Result cache.** One writer per partition image; other tabs cannot mutate a receipt-proven image |
| Global search over 1,000 memberships costs ~1,010 HTTP requests through a client N-way merge | LLP 1024 §2 | **Result cache** (no local index over the partition) + **static pricing** (the server-side projection is refused) |
| The complete local runtime grew 7.8 KB → 89,843 gzip bytes; the ceiling was raised 34,000 → 50,000 → 66,000 → 82,000 → 82,500 → 84,000 → 85,000 → 90,000 | LLP 1000 §"Revised"; `rules/NOT-DOING.md` §Moves; LLP 1018.008 §10 | **Result cache.** The inputs list of `metrics/twin.jsonl` is coverage spans, extent facts, held directories, posting trees, fact integrity, swap journals — receipt machinery, not the interpreter |
| `Snapshot` carries `data, next, partial, receipt{state, source, seq, next, reason, rule, sites}, freshness{live, staleSince, connection}, local{predicted, retained, caughtUp, watermark}, refusal, status, reason` | LLP 1018.003 §1 | Both. Every field is a true fact about the mechanism; almost none is a fact about the application |
| `Schema::rule_hash` binds every queued write to the complete schema; an optional column on the subject table invalidates unrelated evidence | LLP 1018.002; LLP 1018.008.008 | **Result cache.** Evidence identity exists because held answers must be re-authorized; a replica re-runs the mutation at the server and needs no evidence identity |

Against that, what scored: on the 2026-09-05 three-scorer Clique run
Snapback 2 beat Convex on authorization (82 vs 79) and offline (81 vs 44)
and lost on queries and live (60 vs 85), client integration (59 vs 79),
setup and docs (55 vs 79), diagnostics and loop (66 vs 77), operations (53
vs 76) and scale (62 vs 80); overall 66 vs 81. The later single-lane 0.0.21
and 0.0.25 scores of 82 and 85 are real improvement and not a three-scorer
comparison. The wins are the core; the losses are composition, discovery,
scale and operations — the substrates and what sits on them.

LLP 2000 §2 summarises this as "costs arise where one layer must reconstruct
another layer's facts." True, and the layer doing the reconstructing is the
client, because it was designed as a cache that may not compute. Name the
cause and the remedy follows.

## 3. Where LLP 2000 is right, and should not be diluted

- **The whole application is the unit.** §3's four properties (composition,
  locality, continuity, performance) are the right acceptance vocabulary.
  Keep them as the headings of the scenario suite.
- **Ownership before abstraction.** §5's table is the best paragraph in the
  document. §8 below turns its fourth row into a fixed vocabulary.
- **Composed reads must not fabricate.** Unknown totals stay unknown; revoked
  rows do not linger. Both flips below preserve this; they change what is
  *known*, not what is *claimed*.
- **Writes have one lifecycle with durable admission distinct from commit
  and stable operation identity.** Keep LLP 1018.004's three outcomes.
- **Rust and SQLite.** Yes, and the engine's transaction, interpreter, rule
  evaluation and successor logic are the pieces to copy first (§5).
- **Prototype the experience before the core.** Yes — and §8 makes the
  prototype's API signatures binding on the implementation, not the reverse.
- **Deterministic simulation early**, citing TigerBeetle. This is the single
  most important sentence in LLP 2000 and §11 makes it the primary harness.
- **A real deployment and a native client early.** Yes; §11 puts the native
  client in slice one.
- **Parallel, fenced, 2000-series, promotion is the author's later call.** Yes.

## 4. Where LLP 2000 is wrong or leaves the wrong things open

### 4.1 It reopens the language and leaves the bound model open; invert that

§6 asks to "evaluate Quarry" and "evaluate any alternative against the same
contracts." The one-language decision took thirty-seven paper applications,
a three-family panel, a spelling spike and an author ruling (LLP
0329.014.004 §9, LLP 1004, LLP 1007 §4b) to close, and the shipped result is
the most readable artifact in Snapback 2: the chat schema is 129 lines and a
query is three to six. Reopening the language will consume the first month.
What *should* be reopened is the bound model under it, which §6 phrases as a
comparison to "concentrate early investigation on." Half of the Quarry
refusals a builder meets are the certifier's byte and step prices wearing
Quarry syntax; the `fn`/`proc` split and the page-to-list gap (LLP 1018.008
F4) are bounded-composition restrictions the pricing model forced. Decide
the bound model (§6), keep the language, and re-derive its restrictions.

### 4.2 The first slice is at the scale where Snapback 2 looked elegant

§10 proposes "a private conversation with paginated messages and an unread
count," "two principals and two clients." Snapback 2 passed that slice in
its first week. It broke at the hub: 1,000 memberships refused the digest
fold, made global search cost a thousand requests, and refused the
envelopes builders first wrote (LLPs 1023, 1024, 1025). Every one of those
was discovered by a cold builder on a published package weeks after the
architecture was fixed. The first slice must include the hub principal from
day one (§11).

### 4.3 It investigates the client data model; the field has answered

§6 says "test a canonical local store with deliberate derived indexes,
durable pending operations, and explicit coverage and authorization
knowledge." That sentence describes a replica; the field has shipped it
under several names (buckets, mutators and a change stream, per-device
queues), Snapback 2's own LLP 1012 §6 reaches it by the hard road ("the
coverage rule that makes everything on the phone exact" is a fully drained
partition), and LLP 1019's `deliver` already computes the audience the
engine must maintain. Decide it (§7) and spend the investigation budget on
the two things the field has *not* settled for this design: partition
inversion of Quarry rules, and horizons at hub scale.

### 4.4 "Builder, human or agent" is two audiences

§3's first row conflates them. Agents need structured refusals with a
# LLP 3000.000.000: Retained storage and hot release activation

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Implemented
**Systems:** Schema evolution, Storage, CLI, Deployment, Replica generations
**Author:** Codex for Charlie Cheever (Workstream ROLL)
**Date:** 2026-10-12
**Revised:** 2026-10-16 (PREV wave-13 preview decisions §13); 2026-10-15 (SURF readiness/closeout; aggregate identity migration covers retained references; PUBFIX28: consistent disjoint preview prose and blocking accepted control streams); 2026-10-14 (NARR wave-11 amendment); 2026-10-13 (§10: REL2 wave-10 release lifecycle); 2026-10-13 (MACFIX: explicit pre-retention compatibility boundary and canonical deployment/worker paths); 2026-10-12 (§5.1: path-independent control socket; implemented; dual-family confirmation, captured lifetimes, native generations, hot activation and complete qualification)
**Related:** [LLP 3000](./3000-snapback4.explainer.md), [LLP 3000.000](./3000.000-atomic-schema-and-release-activation.research.md), [LLP 3000.007](./3000.007-relaxed-quarry-and-explicit-residency.rfc.md), [LLP 3000.019](./3000.019-what-the-builder-still-wrote.rfc.md)

Staging store classification and real-user provisioning: [LLP 3000.000.000.001](./3000.000.000.001-staging-with-real-users.rfc.md) (STAGE).

## 1. Commission and scope

Charlie approved the ideal end-game release lifecycle and commissioned implementation on 2026-10-12. Implementer: this ROLL session, beginning that date. Wave 8 DT1 found additive rollback deletes new storage in all 50 day-two lanes; DT1a found unnamed enum conflicts; DT10 found deployment requires stopping serve. This supersedes the release-only removal semantics in LLP 3000.000's implementation follow-up. Development and device replacement keep their existing projection/reset semantics. No new publication authority or gate is introduced.

## 2. Retain by default

Every release activation (deploy and rollback) retains removed durable application tables, columns and index definitions in SQLite-owned dormant storage within the same activation transaction. The active r/i image remains an exact projection of the active schema. Dormant data is outside that namespace and unavailable to programs, rules, references, queries, sync, SQL application projections and clients. The Quarry/IR binder admits only declared application table names; it has no general application SQL evaluator. Dormant internal names are not added to Schema, and schema validation forbids declarations in the reserved `_dormant_` and `_release_` namespaces. Inspection `snapback4 sql` is owner-only and may enumerate it. A future application SQL adapter must keep its declared authorized input allow-list. Asset reads must resolve the home table, column and row in the active schema/r image and check the current read rule; a dormant home refuses E_ASSET_UNKNOWN even when a caller knows its id. Test the reserved-name/program boundary and direct fetch by id. Reintroducing a dormant name deliberately reactivates its data, after full validation under the candidate schema. A deployment that reuses any dormant name refuses unless --restore-dormant explicitly selects restoration; its refusal lists retained items/counts/samples and the alternatives `deploy <dir> --restore-dormant` or `prune <dir> --allow-destructive` before fresh creation. Rollback's selection of an immutable older release explicitly selects restoration; `rollback <dir> --to N` also selects an immutable release for deliberate roll forward. To convert durable to ephemeral, deploy a release without that table, prune it explicitly, then deploy the ephemeral definition. This applies to deployments as well as rollback; a rename remains two names, without an inferred data move.

Inventory and audit entries carry a stable typed key (`table:name`, `column:table.name`, or `index:table.name`); pagination uses the returned key, not a bare path that could collide between columns and indexes. The SQLite format is `_dormant_tables(table_name, definition)` for whole-table Table definitions, `_dormant_rows(t,id,data)` for their stored images, `_dormant_columns(table_name,column_name,definition)` for retired column type/default/ownership, `_dormant_values(t,id,column_name,data)` for their cells, `_dormant_indexes(table_name,index_name,definition)` for index definitions and `_dormant_refs(t,id,column_name,parent_t,parent_id,invalid)` for deletion evidence with a parent lookup index. Catalog numbers are reserved monotonically and reused for the same physical name, but dormant rows are never kept in r under a hidden catalog number. These tables are created only during successful release activation; legacy deployments start empty and cannot recover data previously deleted by older binaries. A successful release activation writes the release-only format marker `version=snapback4-release-store-3`. New Store open/version checks accept this, the prior snapback4-release-store-2 and the legacy snapback4-store-1; old binaries reject it with E_STORE rather than opening it. Development and device stores retain snapback4-store-1 and their formats are unchanged. The format marker rolls back with failed activation. On restore, omitted cells from whole-table images move to dormant column cells before projection, merging previous retired cells; full-table entries are consumed only on commit.

The compatibility boundary is the store format, independently of schema
capabilities. Published 0.2.33 refuses retained release-store versions 2 and 3
with `E_STORE: store version <version> is not snapback4-store-1; this data
directory belongs to another Snapback` (the read-only doctor form omits
the final data-directory explanation), including capability-free releases.
That refusal must preserve committed state and never interpret dormant data
under the old layout. Capability-free backend/schema bytes and unchanged
store-1 development/device fixtures retain their separate compatibility
controls (LLP 3000.022 §14.3); they do not establish old-server readability of
new release stores. The Darwin publication fence checks both boundaries.
The MACFIX compatibility/path amendment passed independent final
[Muse](./reviews/3000.000.000-macfix-r3.muse.md) and
[Codex](./reviews/3000.000.000-macfix-r3.codex.md) reviews at e9150bd171.
The required Darwin schema and worker files passed alone with default TMPDIR
(10 and 82 tests); whole-publication qualification is a separate claim.

Dormant whole tables store their row images; dormant columns store values keyed by table/column/row id and their definitions. Capture interpreted literal defaults and optional nulls when a column becomes dormant, preserving the value existing rows actually saw. Keep index definitions as history; preserve their data logically and rebuild physical keys on reactivation from current rows. Dormant indexes are never consulted for active writes, uniqueness or caps. Thus a new duplicate under the older release can precisely refuse forward activation. Derived rows are caches and are rebuilt, not authoritative history. Retention must precede existing derived-state retirement. An unsupported durable-to-ephemeral conversion refuses rather than silently discarding stored data.

Partial restoration consumes only candidate-visible cells: B's T(a,b) → absent T → C's T(a) restores a and moves b to dormant cells, with b's definition, then B restores b. For an identical (t,id,column) cell key, the newly retired active value wins: it is the most recent authoritative value. Preserve other dormant keys. Use transactional upsert and test retire → restore → update → retire → restore. Keep zero-row table/column/index definitions too. Whole-table restoration first restores its stored image, splits omitted cells into dormancy, then projects the active image. Consumption shares commit with validation. Refuse application-table to derived-table ownership changes, or authoritative-column to maintained/captured ownership changes, including when the application table/column was dormant. Derived caches never overwrite retained authoritative data. The refusal names the incompatible owner and directs a deliberate rename or prune while dormant.

At retirement record reference edges for dormant rows/cells (including optional/list references), bound to their current parent lifetime. An active delete transaction marks matching dormant edges invalid before the id can be reused, even for identical replacement values. Only a move into dormant storage or replacement of a derived cache is exempt from deletion invalidation. Actual authoritative-row deletion always invalidates matching dormant edges, including expiry during activation; failed activation rolls the invalidation back. A fixed-size identity migration rewrites dormant whole rows, cells, narrowed rows and their edge ledger when the referenced derived target lifetime survives migration; removing that target retires its lifetime, so its dormant edges remain bound to the retired address and restoration refuses rather than attaching them to a later reintroduction. Restoration refuses invalidated edges or missing parents with table/column/parent ids, counts and samples; it cannot silently recapture another parent's audience. For read-through rows, compare preserved captured audience and identity fields to the candidate parent before existing recapture work. A changed audience refuses restoration; inherited deadlines keep their original capture. Newly expired rows remain logically invisible and may be swept only under the reactivated schema. Test deletion/reuse, identical replacement, changed audience, nested references and expiry. This adds no device incarnation format.

Restoration, candidate validation, derived rebuild, catalog/generation/programs and current release commit together. Any error rolls all of them back. Retained values overwrite no explicit candidate writes: restoration precedes activation builders. Incompatible name reuse must pass candidate types and constraints; no flag bypasses validation.

## 3. Older writes and incompatible values

Updating an active row while its newer columns are dormant preserves those dormant values. Deleting that row deletes its dormant column values in the same transaction: forward never resurrects a deleted row, including when its id is reused. New rows written under the older schema have no dormant value. On forward they receive the candidate literal default or optional null; required columns without such a default refuse with exact missing-row counts and up to five ids. Repair by deploying a bridge schema with that column optional or a literal default using --restore-dormant, fill it through a bridge mutation, then deploy the desired required schema. Alternatively explicitly delete the named rows through the current release and retry. Never tell the older schema to write a field it lacks. Contextual now/viewer defaults do not invent historical values. Existing required-column backfill rules still apply.

Release activations retain incompatible existing values per row rather than refusing a type narrowing. Section 9 specifies the visibility, reference and restoration rules. Development adoption keeps its checked narrowing behavior. Missing newly required fields still need the bridge described above; retention applies to present historical values, not absent backfill. No implicit coercion or fallback is introduced.

For every data-dependent refusal, aggregate count and bounded samples (five distinct values/keys, 256 UTF-8 bytes each, plus truncation markers) at the failed column/index/constraint, without unbounded row retention. Include actual conflicting values for column failures, index name and conflicting keys for unique failures, and actionable command or schema repair. Detailed values are privileged operator diagnostics only, never ordinary application refusal payloads, query meter diagnostics or sync refusals. No claim that a green read-only preview freezes later writes: adoption rechecks under its transaction.

## 4. Explicit pruning and audit

snapback4 prune <dir> runs through the serving owner when live, otherwise the offline owner. It lists dormant tables, columns and indexes with row counts, logical serialized dormant bytes (including retained asset bytes separately) and at most five ids; it changes nothing without --allow-destructive. With that flag it permanently deletes exactly the currently dormant storage in one writer transaction, records a durable prune event, and leaves active rows/programs untouched. It releases dormant asset homes for the ordinary collector. Deploy/rollback --allow-destructive do not prune; direct users to the separate command. Prune intentionally deletes all dormant items as one reviewed storage boundary; per-item dependency-aware pruning is deferred because partial pruning can strand dormant references and asset homes. There is no implicit size or age collector: previews make storage growth visible without inventing a data-loss policy. After prune, reintroducing the schema gets empty tables and normal required-column/default behavior. Pruning is an irreversible operator action; the command output names every item dropped.

Release activation events live in the database transaction with activation revision, operation, release/previous/generation, timestamp and retained/restored summary. Prune records its item summary in that transaction. Existing releases.jsonl and current remain checked convenience copies; post-commit filesystem errors explicitly say activation succeeded. History is never edited or rewound. Rollback picks an earlier immutable source, not a historical data snapshot.

Dormant bytes have no automatic age-based deletion: retention lasts until explicit prune, and prune preview exposes populations so operators control disk use. Dormant asset homes remain live in the asset ledger but are unreadable because the active schema has no home. Active-row deletion also retires any associated dormant asset home. Dormant rows do not run old expiry or jobs while dormant; candidate liveness applies again upon reactivation. Explicit application deletion and ordinary active expiry still mean deletion, not an undo archive.

## 5. Hot activation and ownership

serve owns the database sidecar for its lifetime and publishes a Unix-domain release-control socket in the private per-user runtime directory (§5.1; directory 0700, socket 0600). Check peer credentials on both ends (Linux SO_PEERCRED, Darwin getpeereid), requiring the effective UID. Only the host filesystem owner has this capability; it is not an HTTP door, browser origin, bearer service seat or remote endpoint. Check filesystem objects before replacing a stale socket; never follow symlinks or replace a regular file. Use `.release-command` (a hardened private regular file, no-follow, single link) for the command flock and the deterministic runtime address (§5.1) for the control socket. lstat socket entries; only a same-owner socket may be removed while holding the exclusive store sidecar. Bound command JSON to 4096 bytes and response JSON to 64 KiB, with a 5-second input timeout and 120-second command response timeout. The trusted same-UID operator can directly edit source/database; malicious same-UID edits are outside the threat model, but ordinary source races are caught by a candidate content digest over source/native/worker files in the request and verified by the server. Numeric release id is the only source selector; never a caller path. Commands hold a separate private command flock while allocating/staging immutable release source. They acquire the store owner when stopped; E_STORE_BUSY with a running serve uses the local socket instead of opening another writer. Dev and an older/unreachable server continue to refuse with an exact stop/retry command.

snapback4 releases <dir> reads authoritative current release, activation revision, generation and durable events without adopting source, including while serve is live. This is the recovery command for an ambiguous result. A bounded command message identifies the owned database inode/canonical identity, candidate release id, expected current release and monotone activation revision, operation and explicit flags. The server compiles only its own immutable release directory, prepares native module/worker prerequisites before commit, then holds its existing engine writer guard for activation and in-memory publication. The server verifies both expected current release and activation revision inside the activation transaction. Increment activation revision on every successful release activation, including an equal-hash source change; A→B→A cannot satisfy an old command. Concurrent commands serialize on the command flock; out-of-band stale commands fail compare-and-swap. No request may choose arbitrary filesystem source. Compilation/validation failure leaves the previous engine, bound schema, native module, worker revision and reader factory unchanged.

A query already running in a pinned SQLite read transaction finishes on its old snapshot and reports that generation. A writer already executing finishes before activation obtains its engine guard. Queued HTTP operations are admitted under one release request guard covering signature lookup, argument decoding and execution. Activation acquires its exclusive side before taking the engine guard; admitted operations finish before activation, and later operations dispatch under the new generation. Internal detached readers still fence obsolete identities with E_GENERATION. Fresh requests after publication use the new engine; a stale pooled reader not yet pinned retries through the replacement factory. Prepare a runtime bundle containing bound programs, precompiled native module/metadata, worker root and validated named declarations; prepare the reader factory from the post-activation engine while its writer guard blocks new writers (reader opening failure after commit clears the old factory and uses existing lazy retry, never aborts a committed activation or advertises the old backend). Swap bound/generation/native under the common bound guard in the existing /schema guard order, retire pooled readers, rebind ephemeral state, invalidate change answers, wake sync/polls and update worker revision while holding the writer guard. The supervisor explicitly switches its immutable release project root and named declarations on revision, including addition/removal/replacement; externally supplied --worker stays an operator-owned entry. Worker preparation captures an immutable (root, entry, declarations/environment, supervisor revision) tuple. Under the engine guard it compares that captured revision to the current worker revision before minting credentials; stale preparation is discarded and rediscovered. Never relabel an old prepared source with the latest revision. Credentials bind that checked revision so later activation fences them even when spawn finishes later. Workers restart using the candidate packaged source and native continuation hashes keep the existing conservative parking policy. Long validation can pause writes; hot means no process restart, not an unbounded zero-pause latency promise.

The server continues an admitted activation even if the client hits its 120-second response timeout or closes the socket; failure to write the reply is not permission to roll back or exit the server. The database is authoritative at crash or lost socket reply. Timeout/EOF after sending a command reports an ambiguous result and instructs inspection of current and durable history; never silently resend as a new release or claim failure left the old release active. Before activation fsync required immutable source/native/worker files and their directories, then the published releases directory entry. The supported guarantee covers process crashes and filesystem/SQLite durability on hosts honoring fsync; no guarantee covers failing storage hardware. Restart attaches the database-selected release even if current's convenience copy is stale. After database commit, publication is infallible in-memory assignments and committed ephemeral rebind; fallible native compilation, declaration/environment/import validation happens before commit. Worker process spawn remains fallible after commit: failure reports committed activation with degraded worker availability and retries; it cannot undo a release or external effects. Filesystem-copy failures report the committed result.

### 5.1 Path-independent control address

The accept loop polls a nonblocking listener, but immediately resets each
accepted stream to blocking mode before peer validation or input. Darwin/BSD
inherits the listener's nonblocking flag; Linux does not. Blocking streams
let the existing total input deadline govern clients that write after accept.
If resetting the stream fails, close it and report the accept error; never
admit a command on a stream with unknown I/O mode.

Amendment commissioned and implemented by Codex for Charlie on 2026-10-12,
for the 0.4.24 macOS publication blocker. Darwin's Unix socket path limit
is 104 bytes; the previous deployment-local name failed under ordinary
`/var/folders/.../T/` fixtures. Linux's descriptor-address workaround did not
solve Darwin or provide one portable transport.

Use `/tmp/snapback4-<effective-uid>/<sha256>.sock` on both hosts. The hash is
SHA-256 over the raw bytes of the canonical **deployment directory**, binding
the socket to immutable release source. Aliases that canonicalize to the same path resolve
to one address; two deployments pointing to one external database retain
separate addresses, so only the serving deployment can submit its releases.
At deploy and serve startup, create a missing deployment privately, resolve
its canonical directory once, then use that directory for ownership and all
release paths. Serve also resolves the selected release root before constructing
the project. Worker cwd and `SNAPBACK_PROJECT_DIR` therefore report the same
canonical active release path, including Darwin's `/var` → `/private/var` and
explicit deployment symlinks. An existing deployment alias resolves before
private-directory validation; no symlink is installed inside private storage.
Use the same canonical path spelling on case/normalization-insensitive hosts;
byte-distinct canonical spellings produce distinct addresses. The request
envelope still binds the canonical database path, device and inode.
The longest address is 95 bytes (ten decimal UID digits), below both limits.
Do not use TMPDIR or XDG_RUNTIME_DIR: these may be long or differ between CLI
invocations. This deterministic address needs no durable locator file; deploy
and rollback derive exactly the same address from durable deployment location.
A moved deployment must stop and restart serve, as moving its immutable source
already requires. Socket entries are ephemeral, never backup authority.

Create the single runtime directory atomically as 0700. Open with O_DIRECTORY
and O_NOFOLLOW, inspect the opened metadata, and refuse a different UID, a
non-directory/symlink or any mode other than 0700 (including special bits).
Never repair or chmod an existing directory. `/tmp` is the platform's shared
sticky temporary root (Darwin may alias it to `/private/tmp`). An untrusted
user may pre-create a conflicting name and cause refusal, but gains no access
and causes no modification of their entry. Malicious same-UID filesystem edits
remain outside §5's threat model.

Bind only while holding the exclusive database sidecar. An existing entry
must be a same-owner 0600 socket. Probe it: a live listener refuses without
unlinking; only connection-refused/not-found permits stale replacement. Any
other error refuses. Preserve the inode check around client connect, same-UID
peer checks on both ends, envelope identity, command flock and revision CAS.
Establish inode-based cleanup custody immediately after bind, before chmod or
nonblocking setup; Drop removes only that owned socket inode. Graceful shutdown
joins control before releasing store ownership. Fatal exit after permission setup leaves a stale
socket for the next exclusive owner. A crash between bind and chmod may leave
a socket with the launching umask's mode; if it is not exactly 0600, recovery
refuses and the operator must remove that stale entry. Do not change the
process-global umask in a multithreaded host to hide this fail-closed window. Keep the shared runtime directory on exit:
removing it would race other deployments, and it contains no durable state.

Acceptance: default-TMPDIR project paths exceeding 108 bytes on Linux and
Darwin start serve, execute hot deploy and rollback without restarting, refuse
a second host, clean up on SIGTERM, and restart after SIGKILL. Unit regressions
cover stale/live socket entries, file/symlink and 0600 socket refusals, directory
permissions/type/UID checks, canonical aliases, independent deployments, and
preserving replaced entries during cleanup. SIGTERM/SIGINT/SIGHUP also drain
an incomplete control request before process exit; join the control handle
while Shared still retains the owner. An admitted activation is allowed to
finish. Enforce the five-second input bound as a total monotonic deadline,
updating the remaining socket read timeout before every read; partial input
arriving repeatedly cannot reset the deadline or prolong shutdown. The signal
regression keeps sending partial input until the host closes the connection.
No budget or public door changes.

## 6. Devices and partitions

Every changed execution activation including A→B→A gets a fresh monotone semantic generation, never reuses a release number as a generation. Execution-content identity includes the prepared native module digest as well as schema/programs. A changed native digest advances the existing semantic generation atomically with release activation, even when native declarations are unchanged; removing a module changes the digest too. Equal execution content retains generation unless a release activation changes the final active/dormant population (§9.1), which requires a new replica generation; source-only activations still advance activation revision. The digest is SHA-256 over the exact prepared wasm bytes; the no-module sentinel is the empty string persisted as release_native_digest. activation_hash binds schema/programs and that digest. Comparison is with the current activation, so A→B→A is fresh. Startup attaches the persisted execution identity and verifies the immutable release module matches its recorded digest. This uses the existing device protocol, not a separate native epoch. Rollback and forward use the same generation replacement and full partition reacquisition already used by adoption: /schema advertises the exact active shape, sync rejects old watermarks, devices clear obsolete base/index state and rebase or refuse their queued intents under the existing authority/operation contract. Store id remains unchanged; this is not a store replacement. No dormant rows or fields enter snapshots, partition logs, online answers or device stores. Never combine old-generation pages with a new backend. A late old read remains tagged old and cannot overwrite a newly observed generation. Simulate rollback/forward across a delayed sync page and audience changes; retained data changes server storage only, not device persistence formats.

Lock order: command flock is CLI-only; serving control never acquires it. Candidate filesystem/native/worker preparation precedes engine lock. Serving publication holds engine → bound → generation → native, and separately replaces readers while engine is held (matching existing checkout); no /schema handler takes engine while holding bound. Ephemeral rebind uses its existing committed infallible path, never a fallible post-commit store read. Update desired worker root before revision; worker thread releases root/bound guards before taking engine. Wake/seq/answers follow existing publish_engine while engine is held: these are short memory updates/nonblocking condition notifications, required before new writer admission; no socket/child wait occurs there. `releases` includes the existing worker status metadata so post-commit launch failure is visible as degraded availability. No SQLite lock is held while waiting for a child process or socket peer.

## 7. Implementation and acceptance

Small commits: reviewed design; transactional retention/prune/refusals; local hot-control publication; integration/device/client coverage and docs. Authoring entry: SERVER.md owns the release-lifecycle section; guide evolution owns schema interactions. OPS guide deploy gets a one-line pointer to that section.

CLI matrix: `deploy <dir> [--restore-dormant] [--allow-feed-adoption]`; `rollback <dir> [--to N] [--allow-feed-adoption]`; `prune <dir> [--allow-destructive]`; `releases <dir>`. Legacy --allow-destructive on deploy/rollback is accepted but does not prune or bypass validation, and prints the prune command when relevant. --restore-dormant authorizes only restoration, never loss. --to requires a positive existing immutable release id and may select a newer release. E_ADOPT operator text: `table.column/index: N affected rows; sample ids [...]; values/keys [...]; reason; rewrite: <bridge/repair schema or command>; inspect: snapback4 sql <query> --release <dir>`. No flag bypasses invalid values, ownership or references. Unreachable live control refuses `E_STORE_BUSY: stop snapback4 serve <dir>, rerun the exact command, then snapback4 serve <dir>` before send; after send use `releases <dir>` and explicit `rollback --to N` after inspecting the outcome, never an automatic deploy allocating N+1.

History/status is page-based with `releases <dir> [--after <event-id>]`; bounded pages return a continuation and inventory pages use stable item keys. Prune audit stores a complete item list in SQLite; bounded replies carry a continuation rather than truncate silently. Critical assertions include T(a,b) → absent T → T(a) → absent T → T(a,b), preserved b and latest updated a; identical parent id/value replacement invalidates dormant edges; stale revision after A→B→A and equal-hash activation refuses; activation-time parent expiry followed by identical-id replacement invalidates dormant references; rapid B→C activation with a barrier between B worker preparation and credential minting discards B conventional/named source; an old checked-out reader not yet pinned retries the new factory including native-only changes; a connected client holding native module A hot-adopts B with unchanged declarations, reacquires B backend/module and refuses a delayed A result; inject interruption before/after commit and during pointer-copy updates to distinguish no activation from committed activation with an ambiguous result.

Tests also cover private socket permissions/symlinks/peer credentials, racing commands and writes during validation, process crash before commit and between commit/publication/reply, live prune/status, legacy empty dormancy and unchanged device format. Tests: deploy v1/write, v2 adding table/required default/enum/index/write, precise refusal while new enum is in use, authorized repair then rollback with dormant table/column, old update/insert/delete, forward restores prior values and supplies defaults only to new rows. Also prune preview/confirmation and asset lifetime; unique failure after dormant-index writes; required default-less forward refusal; transaction failure leaves active and dormant data intact; restart binds the committed release; simultaneous hot requests and failed hot activation; stale-generation sync and real client reacquisition; simulator delayed pages/audiences. Quality bar: the commissioned core/lang/device/sim cargo suites, CLI release/serve/adoption tests, relevant client tests and ref-check. No data-loss claim rests on diagnostics alone.

## 8. Review disposition and continuation

Four initial revisions/review rounds are recorded verbatim under llp/reviews/. Muse returned READY on revision 4. Codex identified actual-deletion evidence, revision-bound worker preparation, and native-only content in semantic generation identity; all are explicit above. The coordinator subsequently authorized targeted confirmation, further review rounds and judgment calls, and commissioned implementation through tests and commits without another approval stop.

Targeted confirmation after continuation approval: both fresh Codex and Muse returned READY. Their verbatim responses are appended to the same provenance artifacts. Implementation follows this revision.

## 9. Implementation record

The core implements dormant tables/cells/index definitions and reference-lifetime evidence, partial restoration, required-value/enum/unique/cap refusal populations, prune/history, release-only format fencing, revision CAS and native digest in activation_hash. Nineteen focused retention regressions pass, including activation-time expiry, assets, typed audit pagination and ownership refusal.

The host implements the private same-owner transport, prepared native/worker custody, atomic live engine publication, reader fencing, revision-bound worker preparation, named-worker mutation and database-authoritative crash recovery. Seven focused hot-activation tests and four transport tests pass; the complete host unit suite passes (393 tests plus four existing ignored). All 23 release CLI/client integration tests pass, including an already-connected native-only client, concurrent traffic, hot narrowing refusal and process death before/after commit. Restoring full tables installs the identity scan before read-through adoption. Historical recorded attribution (including nulls), parent bindings and captured deadlines survive restoration; only physically missing captures are filled, and effective deadlines recompute under the candidate. An older release may make a through reference mutable; restoration refuses if its current parent table/id differs from the retained binding. Both naming orders of joint parent/child retirement, an unswept expired attribution target, effective deadline visibility, partial capture deletion/reuse and older retargeting are regressions. A test-only publication barrier permits an owned subprocess kill after commit and before runtime/pointer/reply publication, followed by database-authoritative recovery and retained forward restoration. Named secret selections are validated in immutable preparation before commit. The device and simulator exercise delayed old snapshots and audiences on both stores. SERVER.md owns the lifecycle semantics, the evolution card supplies the schema bridge, and the OPS deploy card is a one-line pointer. Final full quality results and commits are recorded in WORKSTREAM-REPORT.md. No publication is claimed.


Final complete core/lang/device/simulator command finished with exit 0. The 159-case consolidated client scope finished with 158 passing, one existing optional 0.2.32 interoperability skip and zero failures against an immutable built CLI. Final CLI, web/package and ref-check results are recorded in WORKSTREAM-REPORT.md. Internal name reservation is limited to `_dormant_` and `_release_`: established application underscore naming remains supported, while catalog-number resolution into r/i isolates physical internal tables. Both independent amendment reviewers returned READY. The implemented RFC is archived by removing its current/ link; this file remains at its permanent path.

## 8. Integration with the loop (2026-10-12)

Referential actions are selected with the active schema (LLP 3000.039). A
cascade/restrict declared only on a retired table/column is inactive while that
storage is dormant. The existing active-delete hook invalidates dormant edges;
restoration refuses E_ADOPT after deletion/reuse instead of applying a retired
action. If the reference remains active, the selected release's action applies,
including its default restrict. Retention cannot bypass active reference validation.

Union audiences (LLP 3000.036.000.000) and merged pages (LLP 3000.036.001) use
the ordinary generation reset and acquisition path. Reordered old pages refuse
E_GENERATION; a membership removed while the group arm is inactive cannot return
on roll forward. Activation's extracted builder preserves the audience-epoch
update for changed group declarations and the calendar schedule validity check.

Browser journal custody (LLP 3000.013.000 §17) remains tied to store and principal,
not release generation. Captured ids survive forward/rollback; current programs
rejudge an unexecuted intent, and receipts replay an executed one at most once.
A login epoch changes only with its authority, while personas retain epoch 0.
Serve refuses personas; the persona browser regression exercises the same
schema-generation changes through dev adoption, in both source directions.
OPS's deploy card retains account/admin/migration/port recipes and points to
SERVER.md#release-lifecycle for hot activation, retention, explicit restore/prune
# LLP 3000.011.003.000.000: Packaged application proof kit

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Implemented
**Systems:** Snapback 4 proof tooling, starters, fixtures, release source selection
**Author:** Codex for Charlie Cheever
**Date:** 2026-10-14
**Revised:** 2026-10-17 (KIT3 proof and clock contracts); 2026-10-16 (REG missing frontend export diagnostic; §11: wave-14 DAY4); 2026-10-16 (§9: wave-13 DAY3; §10: wave-13 KIT2); 2026-10-16 (§8: explicit platform boundary and client-source guard); 2026-10-15 (§8: wave-12 KIT and observational listener qualification)
**Related:** [Starters](./3000.011.003.000-offline-ready-application-starters.rfc.md), [Release activation](./3000.000.000-retained-storage-and-hot-release.rfc.md), [Built application proof](./3000.011.005.000-qualify-the-built-application.rfc.md)

STAGE staging and persistent startDev defaults are amended by [LLP 3000.000.000.001](./3000.000.000.001-staging-with-real-users.rfc.md); KIT4 owns other ongoing helper changes.

## 1. Contract

Implementer: PROOF workstream, 2026-10-14. Wave 11 fix list 1 authorizes
one published `snapback4/proof` Node module. It owns orchestration, never client
custody or backend/bundler supervision in the product. Existing `startDev(args)`
and `stopChild(child | {url, stop})` recipes remain usable as module exports.

`startDev`, `startServe(directory)`, `startStatic(directory)` and
`startVite(directory)` return `{url, pid, logs, stop}`. Options carry cwd,
environment, arguments and bounded ready/stop deadlines. Read PORT_LO/PORT_HI
at invocation: absent means an OS-assigned port; exactly one set refuses. With
both set, validate 1 <= LO <= HI <= 65535 and bind the first free port in that
inclusive range; exhaustion refuses. Qualification's occupied first port must
be skipped, never adopted. The existing native SNAPBACK4_READY line already names its direct child PID (LLP 3000.011.004.000); no new readiness log is needed. The listening process binds first and keeps that socket;
never adopt an already-serving port. Vite uses its supported JavaScript server
API in a directly spawned Node child, strict ports, the project's config and
a per-launch identity middleware. The directly spawned frontend calls its
server listen API for each candidate; no probe-and-release step exists. With
no range it calls listen on port 0, which the tested Vite API supports with
strictPort; it reads the actual retained socket address. Static hosting uses a directly spawned Node
child with traversal protection, correct wasm MIME and SPA fallback.

All child stdout/stderr go to separate files beneath `logs/`. Read complete
lines from those files, strip ANSI, bound readiness, and reject premature exit.
Native ready lines must name the directly spawned PID. To assert HTTP identity,
the kit sets a random SNAPBACK4_PROOF_ID only in the child environment;
`/auth/config` includes `proof_id` only when this variable is nonempty. It
confers no authority or bypass, and ordinary servers omit it. Both static and Vite frontend child
responses carry an `x-snapback4-proof` marker. Identity validation
precedes returning a handle. No public development door is enabled in serve.

Register every spawned child and browser process for an exit-time SIGKILL
sweep, using retained native ChildProcess handles, not process-name kills.
TERM followed by bounded KILL/reap stops a child. Every cleanup action has
its own bound and later actions still run when an earlier action fails.
`cli(args)` runs the native binary, awaits close, returns separate stdout and
stderr, and has a command deadline. It never runs npm/npx or shell strings.

`withBrowser({executablePath: process.env.CHROME}, callback)` launches Chromium
through playwright-core, closes with a bound, and registers its actual browser
process. Root needs --no-sandbox; other hosts first attempt a sandboxed launch
and retry without it only on recognized sandbox-unavailable diagnostics: "No usable sandbox", "Running as
root without --no-sandbox", "Failed to move to new namespace", "SUID sandbox",
"sandbox ... Operation not permitted", or "Failed to unshare ... Operation not
permitted". Unrelated failures never retry.
`waitForFresh` polls for nonempty changed text; `fillRN` clears and types real
keystrokes; `statusText` uses text selectors; `selectByValue` selects stable
option values. The browser executable is CHROME (when provided), undefined executablePath is omitted from launch options, then Playwright's
installed Chromium is used; an absent executable refuses at launch, with its path.
waitForFresh defaults to 30 seconds. Binary resolution is resolveBinary from
the same installed snapback4 package (override SNAPBACK4_BIN only for explicit
workspace tests); the opt-in identity requires CLI/client 0.4.28 or newer and
an older CLI omits proof_id and therefore fails the exact marker assertion; no inferred version comparison or success claim is made. cli(args) does not
invoke package managers; publication uses its existing guarded command runner.

None interprets an HTTP acknowledgement as rendered settlement.

## 2. Explicit source and aliases

`deploy dir --schema file` compiles exactly that saved Quarry file and stages
those same bytes as canonical snapback/schema.q, preserving operation names
regardless of the saved file's name. Compile diagnostics name the selected input.
This happens without swapping the working schema or generating its types.
It does not concatenate other snapback/*.q files. It allocates ordinary numeric
immutable releases, uses the same .pending-adoption marker, transaction/history,
latest selection, restoration-intent preview and no-op checks as normal deploy;
no alternate activation path or restoration bypass exists. The normal deployment path
retains multi-file behavior and activation semantics. Server/native companion source comes from the current project, while all
schema-dependent generated signatures and native builds run against the selected
schema in a private snapshot. A uniquely created snapshot project has
snapback/schema.q, captured companion source, fresh snapback/generated/
and copied native sources (target caches excluded). Host-installed node_modules
is linked into the private project; package.json preserves host module policy.
The snapshot is RAII-owned and removed on success or error; nothing is executed
through the working generated types. Its server entry is typechecked against
the fresh generated server signature before activation; its native crate builds
against fresh generated native.rs into its own native-target directory. Never reuse the working generated signature or
write selected-schema types there. Package prepared signatures and native bytes
with the captured saved source; retain the original host package installation
for dependency resolution. Standalone means no Project::locate result from the current directory/ancestors; that saved source has no companion app. Native snapshot manifests preserve their source layout and rebase only Cargo
dependency paths against the original native crate's directory. TOML parsing
covers normal, build/dev, target-specific, patch/replace and local workspace
dependency maps. Absolute paths stay equivalent; missing dependencies refuse
before compilation. Never rewrite the package's own lib/bin paths to the
working tree; those must compile against the selected private generated types.
 Missing/stale external server
import graphs fail private checking with an actionable TypeScript diagnostic. Keep saved variants outside snapback/ to avoid normal
multi-file compilation. Rollback selects an existing immutable release, never
reads the current schema. The proof `deploy` helper requires a schema path;
`rollback` requires an explicit target; `testSource` requires a source path.
`test --source file` uses the selected bytes in a memory engine (optionally
with fixtures), so it cannot adopt a working store. Diagnostics name the file.

Fixture alias arguments and assertions follow [REL3 §5](./3000.000.000.000-release-spanning-clients-and-development-contracts.rfc.md#5-fixtures-and-dev-evidence). Protocol request decoding never sees this saved-name map and accepts all ordinary literal data unchanged. Saved aliases remain explicit `$name` / `$name.field`. In saved-value argument positions (typed by fixture contracts §7), a bare string
matching an earlier save alias refuses with the alias name and `$name` rewrite
before wire decoding. Text/enum/JSON values and assertion strings stay literal. Unknown `$name` refuses by exact name lookup, never fuzzy matching.
`$name.field` requires every field in the saved object, refusing by the missing
field's name (a non-record has no fields). A `$` appearing inside ordinary text
is literal. `$$text` escapes to the literal `$text`; `{"$literal": value}`
in a fixture argument escapes any value, including an exact bare alias name. This affects only fixture case argument substitution, not protocol decoding.
Fixture preflight/sweep excludes explicitly saved arguments as before.

## 3. Starter and qualification

Web/Expo init emits frontend:proof and day2:proof (the latter takes two saved
source paths as arguments); both use the kit. The additional command(binary,
args) helper applies cli's same direct-child/log/deadline contract to external
build/test tools, without invoking a shell. Web/Expo init emits frontend:proof on the kit and declares playwright-core.
The already-shipped ?api= override is not introduced or expanded here. Proofs
use it on an owned loopback frontend and fresh browser context; production URL
policy belongs to the existing client/starter surface. The user explicitly
assigns source flags, fixture aliases, frontend proofs and their manifest entries
to PROOF; the two lane-owned text areas below remain separate.

The proof starts a memory dev and built static output, passes the actual URL
as ?api=, signs in through the generated screen (social then opens its Profile
tab, where Sign out lives) and drives a visible form when
the default todos schema is present. The fresh terminal receipt precedes the
rendered row assertion. Adapt writes missing proof files and manifest entries (no package-manager command) and keeps
application-owned proof files/scripts. At init, a complete PORT_LO/PORT_HI range is baked like --port-range;
later runtime ranges override the written defaults through launchers. Explicit
--port remains a deliberate client/interactive default. The proof kit still
allocates its own runtime URLs.

Linux publication qualification runs frontend:proof twice for every supported
frontend/template pair: web Todos, web Todos --online, Expo Todos, web chat,
Expo chat and Expo social. Every attempt uses an occupied first port and bounded
CPU load. Retain all twelve exits, log paths and runtime ranges in its receipt; any failed attempt fails qualification. Test
occupied/exhausted ranges, wrong identities, ANSI/blank ready lines, early death,
command close/drained output, browser flags, cleanup escalation, exit sweeps,
saved-source isolation and alias refusals. Three copied wave-11 proofs (018-day2-cross-country-team-splits, 020-day2-golf-tee-time-lottery and 057-service-status-page, copied from ~/sb4-loop/wave11/apps into /tmp/sb4-w11-proof-lanes) retain
their application assertions while replacing orchestration; record line counts
and execution results. Guide testing replaces orchestration recipes with the
kit and four rules: file logs; fresh signals; receipts outside unmounting
components; HTTP acknowledgement is not React settlement.

NARR owns deploy/rollback narration; DX3 owns general client recipes and discovery.

## 4. Custody scope and review disposition

The registered process set uses retained handles. Static and Vite Node hosts
start in private Unix groups and launch a live Node keeper in that same group.
The host's stdin is a driver-owned liveness pipe; driver death including SIGKILL
produces EOF and terminates the host. The keeper's stdin belongs only to the
host. Host exit (including SIGKILL)
closes it; the keeper signals its own group with SIGKILL. The driver gives the host only the read end of its liveness pipe; the keeper
likewise receives only its pipe's read end. Parent write ends are close-on-exec
and never appear in child stdio or IPC descriptors; compiler stdin is its own
pipe, never these write ends. Thus descendant inheritance cannot retain a
liveness writer after its owner dies. The keeper's live membership
pins the group identity, so no observer signals a recycled negative PID.
Vite closes its compiler before normal host exit; the keeper sweeps anything
remaining after either normal or forced exit. On Windows, direct-child cleanup
remains supported; private Unix-group custody is not claimed. Browser processes
use Playwright's process ownership and close before forced stop. Native servers use their existing SNAPBACK4_SUPERVISOR_PID parent-death contract
with the proof driver's PID and already own worker cleanup. Arbitrary detached application processes are outside
this module's supported custody. Publication's existing live-keeper groups remain
the outer command owner and enforce drained groups after each proof, including
abrupt driver death. No process-name or unrelated PID kills are allowed.

## 5. Exact helper defaults

Each launch uses an independent crypto.randomUUID marker. Native children receive
that marker as SNAPBACK4_PROOF_ID; the parent requires exact equality in
/auth/config.proof_id. Frontend children receive their launch UUID, and the
parent requires the exact pid:UUID header, never mere presence. Children in a
scenario need not share a marker: independent markers prove each handle's owner.
Native hosts receive --port LO-HI (or --port 0); only the child binds and prints
its actual loopback URL. The frontend child binds internally, never by a dropped
probe. Defaults: ready 30 s; TERM grace 5 s; KILL reap 5 s; CLI/command 120 s;
each cleanup 10 s; locator/navigation/fresh signal 30 s; browser/context close
5 s each. Log files have fresh UUID names below cwd/logs, never reused.

startServe serves an immutable deployed backend directory; startStatic serves
built frontend files, not a database. command uses a caller-selected executable
and argv (normal spawn PATH lookup when not absolute), shell:false semantics,
file logs and close/deadline checks; cli uses the package resolver. Use direct
tool binaries, never npm/npx to launch proof servers. Standard starter proof
| `snapback4-darwin-arm64` | `sha512-vrf5b0eMdbBsRB9rV92iplz5rlQzdyqFDLJuk79ZIA9YAKka/DETYiEVyYcZcn0ICDiem8QbsJs0U/9qkctGJA==` |
| `snapback4-linux-x64` | `sha512-QcgY0/C/dwxXRMhSbxELx7XT2/5xrnDMQn4NyjvdabpN/6scJjm03VyeCr2Qy2f5H5WBwFWaam9uQ2jjyE6+0Q==` |

+// Execute the packed staging guide verbatim; production refuses bulk setup.
+import assert from "node:assert/strict";
+import { mkdirSync, writeFileSync } from "node:fs";
+import { join, resolve } from "node:path";
+import { spawnSync } from "node:child_process";
+import { runToolchainCommand } from "./publication-starters.mjs";
+
+export async function proveStaging(client, platform, directory) {
+  const cwd = join(resolve(directory), "staging-recipe"); mkdirSync(cwd, { recursive: true });
+  const env = { ...process.env, npm_config_audit: "false", npm_config_fund: "false" };
+  delete env.SNAPBACK4_BIN; delete env.PORT_LO; delete env.PORT_HI;
+  const run = (cmd, args, extra = {}) => runToolchainCommand(cmd, args, { cwd, env: { ...env, ...extra }, timeout: 300_000 });
+  writeFileSync(join(cwd, "package.json"), '{"private":true,"type":"module"}');
+  await run("npm", ["install", "--ignore-scripts", "--no-audit", "--no-fund", resolve(client), resolve(platform)]);
+  const cli = join(cwd, "node_modules/snapback4/bin/snapback4.js");
+  const guide = spawnSync(process.execPath, [cli, "guide", "staging", "--full"], { cwd, env, encoding: "utf8", timeout: 30000 });
+  // The source of every executed recipe is the
+  // installed binary's card, never this checkout's authoring text.
+  assert.equal(guide.status, 0, guide.stderr);
+  const text = guide.stdout;
+  assert.equal(typeof text, "string");
+  const schema = [...text.matchAll(/```quarry\n([\s\S]*?)\n```/g)].map(match => match[1]);
+  const scripts = [...text.matchAll(/```js\n([\s\S]*?)\n```/g)].map(match => match[1]);
+  assert.equal(schema.length, 1); assert.equal(scripts.length, 2);
+  mkdirSync(join(cwd, "snapback")); writeFileSync(join(cwd, "snapback/schema.q"), schema[0]);
+  await run(process.execPath, [cli, "check"]);
+  writeFileSync(join(cwd, "staging.mjs"), scripts[0]); writeFileSync(join(cwd, "first-admin.mjs"), scripts[1]);
+  await run(process.execPath, ["staging.mjs", "--once"]);
+  await run(process.execPath, [cli, "deploy", "production"]);
+  await run(process.execPath, ["first-admin.mjs"], { FIRST_ADMIN_EMAIL: "chosen-admin@example.test", FIRST_ADMIN_PASSWORD: "explicit-production-password" });
+  writeFileSync(join(cwd, "verify.mjs"), verify);
+  await run(process.execPath, ["verify.mjs"]);
+  return { status: "passed", guide: "staging", recipes: "verbatim installed card", roles: { admin: 2, member: 2 },
+    mode: "serve", realPasswordLogin: "passed", data: "passed", selfGrant: "refused", productionBulkSeed: "refused before mint", productionRelabel: "refused", firstAdmin: "passed", privateCredentials: "0600" };
+}
+
+const verify = String.raw`
+import assert from "node:assert/strict";
+import {readFileSync, statSync} from "node:fs";
+import {startServe, seedAccounts, cli} from "snapback4/proof";
+import {login} from "snapback4/auth";
+const plan = {roles:{admin:2,member:2},provision:{name:"provision"}};
+const sessions=JSON.parse(readFileSync("staging-users.json","utf8"));
+assert.equal(statSync("staging-users.json").mode & 0o777,0o600);
+let host=await startServe("staging");
+const post=async(path,token,args={})=>(await fetch(host.url+path,{method:"POST",headers:{"content-type":"application/json",authorization:"Bearer "+token},body:JSON.stringify({args}),signal:AbortSignal.timeout(10000)})).json();
+try {
+ const config=await fetch(host.url+"/auth/config").then(r=>r.json());
+ assert.equal(config.development,false); assert.equal(config.devDoors,false); assert.equal(config.environment,"staging");

+  }

+ assert.equal(minted,false);

+console.log("packed staging: serve, real users/data, first admin, production refusal passed");
+`;
